[32m# Quantifying the Improvement of GRURNTN and LSTMRNTN Over Prior Language Models: An Analysis of Penn Treebank Perplexity Results

[0m
[32m## Introduction

[0m
[32mThe document under review reports the results of a word-level language modeling experiment on the Penn Treebank (PTB) dataset, in which two proposed recurrent architectures — GRURNTN and LSTMRNTN — are evaluated against two architecturally matched internal baselines (GRURNN and LSTMRNN) and against six previously published models ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The source presents test-set perplexity (PPL) figures for every model, alongside a qualitative account of validation-set learning dynamics. This report answers the central question: **how much improvement do the introduced models achieve compared to previous models?** The analysis distinguishes between two comparison classes — (a) the paper's own baselines, and (b) previously published third-party models — and quantifies each margin in both absolute and relative terms, using only the figures supplied by the source ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Scope and Method of This Review

[0m
[32mAll quantitative statements in this report are derived exclusively from the figures reported in the single source document. Where the source supplies a percentage, that figure is reproduced directly; where it does not, the absolute difference and the relative (percentage) reduction are computed from the reported perplexity values so that the reader can see the full size of every margin rather than only the headline comparisons. No external benchmark data, no parameter counts, and no statistical significance tests are available in the source, so the analysis is strictly descriptive of the reported point estimates ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Performance Metric and Experimental Setup

[0m
[32m### The Penn Treebank word-level task

[0m
[32mThe reported experiment concerns word-level language modeling on PTB, a standard benchmark in which a model assigns probabilities to sequences of words ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The source frames the task around test-set perplexity, which is the quantity tabulated for every model compared.

[0m
[32m### Perplexity as the evaluation metric

[0m
[32mPerplexity is reported for all models, and the source treats lower values as better throughout: improvements are described as "reductions," and the best model is identified as the one "which had a consistently lower PPL than the other models" ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Accordingly, a reduction in PPL from one model to another constitutes an improvement, and the magnitude of that reduction — in absolute points and in percentage terms — is the natural measure of the gain.

[0m
[32m### Models under comparison

[0m
[32mThe comparison set consists of four groups ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)):

[0m
[32m1. **Classical and early neural baselines**: N-Gram (141), RNNLM without dynamic evaluation (124.7), and RNNLM with dynamic evaluation (123.2).
[0m
[32m2. **Intermediate recurrent baselines**: SCRNN (115), sRNN (110.0), and DOT(S)-RNN (107.5).
[0m
[32m3. **The paper's own baselines**: GRURNN (97.78) and LSTMRNN (108.26).
[0m
[32m4. **The proposed models**: GRURNTN (87.38) and LSTMRNTN (96.97).

[0m
[32m## Improvements of the Proposed Models Over Their Own Baselines

[0m
[32m### GRURNTN versus GRURNN

[0m
[32mThe source reports that GRURNTN reduced perplexity from 97.78 to 87.38, a reduction of **10.40 PPL in absolute terms** and **10.63% in relative terms** ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). This direction of change is unambiguous: the proposed gated recurrent unit with a tensor-network component improves on its architecturally matched baseline on the same test set under the same experimental conditions described by the source.

[0m
[32m### LSTMRNTN versus LSTMRNN

[0m
[32mThe second proposed model reduced perplexity from 108.26 to 96.97, a reduction of **11.29 PPL absolute** and **10.42% relative** ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Notably, this is the **larger absolute gain of the two**, driven by the weaker starting point of the LSTM baseline (108.26 versus 97.78 for GRURNN). The two models therefore produce near-identical *relative* gains (10.63% and 10.42%) despite different absolute starting points ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Aggregate view of the two within-paper gains

[0m
[32m| Comparison | Baseline PPL | Proposed PPL | Absolute reduction | Relative reduction |
[0m
[32m|---|---|---|---|---|
[0m
[32m| GRURNTN vs. GRURNN | 97.78 | 87.38 | 10.40 | 10.63% |
[0m
[32m| LSTMRNTN vs. LSTMRNN | 108.26 | 96.97 | 11.29 | 10.42% |
[0m
[32m| Mean of the two comparisons | — | — | 10.85 | 10.53% |

[0m
[32mSource: ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32mAveraged across the two architecture families, the proposed modifications deliver a reduction of roughly **10.85 PPL, or about 10.53% relative**, over matched baselines — a consistent, in-family improvement rather than an isolated win in a single configuration ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Head-to-head comparison of the two proposed models

[0m
[32mThe two proposed models are not equivalent in strength. GRURNTN (87.38) is **9.59 PPL lower** than LSTMRNTN (96.97), a **9.89% relative advantage** ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The source states directly that "GRURNTN outperformed all the baseline models as well as the other models by a large margin" ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Consequently, within the paper's own contribution set, the GRU-based variant of the proposed method is the superior configuration, and the LSTM-based variant is the more modest improvement.

[0m
[32m### Relationship between the improved LSTM model and the GRU baseline

[0m
[32mThe source also makes an important cross-family observation: LSTMRNTN "improved the LSTMRNN model and its performance closely resembles the baseline GRURNN" ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Quantitatively, LSTMRNTN (96.97) actually edges past GRURNN (97.78) by **0.81 PPL**, a **0.83% relative** margin. Framed as gap closure, the 11.29-point improvement of LSTMRNTN against its own 108.26 baseline eliminates 107.7% of the 10.48-point gap that separated LSTMRNN from GRURNN — that is, the proposed LSTM variant not only closes the architectural deficit but slightly exceeds the competing baseline ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Improvements of the Proposed Models Over Previously Published Models

[0m
[32m### Full comparison against prior published results

[0m
[32m| Model | Test PPL | GRURNTN advantage (absolute) | GRURNTN advantage (relative) | LSTMRNTN advantage (absolute) | LSTMRNTN advantage (relative) |
[0m
[32m|---|---|---|---|---|---|
[0m
[32m| N-Gram | 141 | 53.62 | 38.03% | 44.03 | 31.23% |
[0m
[32m| RNNLM (w/o dyn. eval) | 124.7 | 37.32 | 29.93% | 27.73 | 22.24% |
[0m
[32m| RNNLM (w/ dyn. eval) | 123.2 | 35.82 | 29.07% | 26.23 | 21.29% |
[0m
[32m| SCRNN | 115 | 27.62 | 24.02% | 18.03 | 15.68% |
[0m
[32m| sRNN | 110.0 | 22.62 | 20.56% | 13.03 | 11.85% |
[0m
[32m| DOT(S)-RNN | 107.5 | 20.12 | 18.72% | 10.53 | 9.79% |
[0m
[32m| LSTMRNN (own baseline) | 108.26 | 20.88 | 19.29% | 11.29 | 10.42% |
[0m
[32m| GRURNN (own baseline) | 97.78 | 10.40 | 10.63% | 0.81 | 0.83% |
[0m
[32m| GRURNTN (proposed) | 87.38 | — | — | −9.59 (GRURNTN is better) | 9.89% |
[0m
[32m| LSTMRNTN (proposed) | 96.97 | 9.59 | 9.89% | — | — |

[0m
[32mPerplexity values are from the source; absolute and relative margins are computed from those values ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Absolute margins

[0m
[32mAgainst the weakest published comparator, the N-Gram model at 141 PPL, GRURNTN achieves a **53.62-point reduction** and LSTMRNTN a **44.03-point reduction** ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Against the strongest published neural comparator, DOT(S)-RNN at 107.5 PPL, the margins narrow but remain substantial: **20.12 points** for GRURNTN and **10.53 points** for LSTMRNTN ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Relative margins

[0m
[32mIn relative terms, GRURNTN's advantage ranges from **38.03%** over N-Gram down to **18.72%** over DOT(S)-RNN, while LSTMRNTN's advantage ranges from **31.23%** down to **9.79%** over the same endpoints ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). This produces a clear, monotone pattern: the stronger the prior model, the smaller the margin — a standard signature of diminishing returns as the baseline quality improves, and an indication that the reported comparisons behave sensibly rather than exhibiting implausible discontinuities.

[0m
[32m### Diminishing returns as baselines strengthen

[0m
[32mThe steepest single drop in margin occurs between the N-Gram model and the RNNLM variants: GRURNTN's relative advantage falls from 38.03% to 29.07% across that step, and LSTMRNTN's falls from 31.23% to 21.29% ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The margin then declines more gradually across SCRNN, sRNN, and DOT(S)-RNN. Importantly, GRURNTN's advantage over the strongest previously published model (18.72%) remains larger than its advantage over its own GRURNN baseline (10.63%), and roughly double that baseline margin in absolute terms (20.12 versus 10.40 PPL) ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Learning Dynamics on the Validation Set

[0m
[32mThe source supplements the test-set table with a per-epoch validation-set comparison ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Three observations are reported:

[0m
[32m- GRURNN "made faster progress than LSTMRNN," indicating a convergence-speed advantage for the GRU baseline within the paper's own experimental setup.
[0m
[32m- GRURNTN's progress was "also better than LSTMRNTN," meaning the proposed GRU variant improved faster during training than the proposed LSTM variant.
[0m
[32m- GRURNTN was "the best model in this task," with "a consistently lower PPL than the other models" across the validation-set epochs ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32mThese findings reinforce the test-set result: GRURNTN's advantage is not attributable to a single favourable evaluation point but appears consistently throughout training, at least at the granularity reported ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Interpretation: How Large Is the Improvement?

[0m
[32mThree conclusions follow from the reported figures, each grounded in the source data.

[0m
[32mFirst, within the paper's own experimental framework, **both proposed models produce consistent, two-digit-percentage relative improvements over their matched baselines** — 10.63% for GRURNTN over GRURNN and 10.42% for LSTMRNTN over LSTMRNN ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Because each proposed model differs from its baseline by a single architectural modification, this consistency across two different recurrent families (GRU-based and LSTM-based) strengthens the inference that the modification — rather than a confound specific to one architecture — drives the gain.

[0m
[32mSecond, **GRURNTN is the single best-performing model reported in the document**, outperforming both the published models and the paper's own baselines by what the source itself characterizes as a large margin ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Its 87.38 PPL is 18.72% better than the best previously published model and 10.63% better than the best internal baseline.

[0m
[32mThird, the improvement against prior published work is **substantial in relative terms but uneven in magnitude**: 18.72%–38.03% for GRURNTN and 9.79%–31.23% for LSTMRNTN depending on the comparator ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The correct takeaway is therefore that the improvement is large relative to the field's earlier entries, but that its size is highly sensitive to which prior model is used as the reference point — a nuance that any headline summary should preserve.

[0m
[32m## Limitations and Caveats

[0m
[32mSeveral limitations constrain how far these numbers can be generalized.

[0m
[32m- **No statistical testing**: The source reports point estimates without variance, confidence intervals, repeated runs, or significance tests, so no claim about the statistical reliability of the reported margins can be verified ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).
[0m
[32m- **A single benchmark**: All evidence comes from the PTB word-level language modeling task; no second dataset is reported, so cross-domain generalization cannot be assessed from the source ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).
[0m
[32m- **Unknown provenance of comparators**: The published models appear as bracketed citation numbers (e.g., [24], [33], [12]) without titles, venues, or years in the supplied text, so the recency and reliability of those comparators cannot be independently evaluated ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).
[0m
[32m- **Table formatting artifacts**: The source table contains stray characters (for example, "0 0 footnotemark: 0" adjacent to the RNNLM rows) and the result table is labelled "TABLE II" although the text refers to "Table I"; these inconsistencies do not alter the numeric values used here but do indicate imperfect transcription ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).
[0m
[32m- **Attribution of gains**: The source attributes the improvements to the proposed tensor-network component by construction, but it does not isolate the effect of individual architectural changes, so the improvement cannot be decomposed into contributions from specific design choices on the basis of this document alone ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Conclusion

[0m
[32mBased on the data provided, the introduced models achieve clear and quantifiable improvements over previous models on PTB word-level language modeling. Against their own matched baselines, GRURNTN reduces perplexity by **10.40 points (10.63%)** relative to GRURNN, and LSTMRNTN reduces perplexity by **11.29 points (10.42%)** relative to LSTMRNN, for an average relative gain of approximately **10.53%** ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). Against previously published work, the strongest proposed model, GRURNTN, lowers perplexity by **18.72% to 38.03%** depending on the comparator, including a 20.12-point (18.72%) reduction relative to the best prior published model, DOT(S)-RNN, and reductions of 24.02% or more relative to N-Gram, RNNLM, and SCRNN ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). LSTMRNTN likewise improves on all published models, with margins from 9.79% to 31.23%, and slightly surpasses the paper's own GRURNN baseline by 0.81 PPL ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The source further reports that GRURNTN maintained a consistently lower validation perplexity throughout training and made faster progress than the other models ([Anonymous, n.d.](document_1.txt)). The improvement is therefore best characterized as **consistent in direction, moderate-to-large in magnitude, and most pronounced for the GRU-based proposed model**, though its generalizability beyond PTB cannot be established from the information supplied.

[0m

[32m# Quantifying the Performance Improvements of GRURNTN and LSTMRNTN over Baseline and Previously Published Language Models on the Penn Treebank Benchmark

[0m
[32m## Introduction

[0m
[32mWord-level language modeling on the Penn Treebank (PTB) benchmark is a long-standing testbed for evaluating recurrent neural network architectures, and perplexity (PPL) is the standard evaluation metric because it summarizes how well a model predicts the next token in a sequence. In this metric, lower values are better, and reductions correspond to more accurate probability assignments over test sequences. The source document analyzed here reports a controlled comparison in which two internally constructed baselines — a gated recurrent unit recurrent neural network (GRURNN) and a long short-term memory recurrent neural network (LSTMRNN) — are evaluated against two proposed variants, GRURNTN and LSTMRNTN, as well as against several previously published systems, including N-Gram, RNNLM with and without dynamic evaluation, SCRNN, sRNN, and DOT(S)-RNN ([Document 1](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32mThe query guiding this report concerns the magnitude of improvement achieved by the introduced models relative to previous models. Answering this question carefully requires separating two distinct comparison classes that the source reports: first, the paired improvements of each proposed model over its own in-house baseline, and second, the improvements of the proposed models over previously published results. The document provides explicit numerical evidence for the first class and a full results table that permits computation of the second class. This report quantifies both, examines the training-dynamics evidence, and assesses the reliability and limitations of the conclusions that can be drawn.

[0m
[32m## Data and Evaluation Framework

[0m
[32m### Benchmark and Metric

[0m
[32mAll figures reported in the source derive from the PTB word-level language modeling task, with test set perplexity as the primary outcome and validation set perplexity per epoch used to characterize learning progress ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). Because perplexity is an exponential function of average negative log-likelihood, even modest absolute reductions are meaningful; double-digit absolute reductions on a benchmark where published systems cluster between roughly 107 and 141 PPL are substantial.

[0m
[32m### Models Compared

[0m
[32mThe comparison set comprises three tiers. The first tier consists of the authors' own baselines: GRURNN at 97.78 test PPL and LSTMRNN at 108.26 test PPL. The second tier contains the proposed models: GRURNTN at 87.38 PPL and LSTMRNTN at 96.97 PPL. The third tier contains previously published results: N-Gram (141), RNNLM without dynamic evaluation (124.7), RNNLM with dynamic evaluation (123.2), SCRNN (115), sRNN (110.0), and DOT(S)-RNN (107.5) ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). The naming convention implies that the "TN" suffix denotes an architectural modification applied to the corresponding baseline unit, which enables clean paired comparisons between GRURNTN and GRURNN, and between LSTMRNTN and LSTMRNN.

[0m
[32m## Quantitative Results

[0m
[32m### Improvements Over the In-House Baselines

[0m
[32mThe source states directly that both proposed models outperformed their respective baselines. GRURNTN reduced perplexity from 97.78 to 87.38, an absolute reduction of 10.40 PPL and a relative reduction of 10.63%. LSTMRNTN reduced perplexity from 108.26 to 96.97, an absolute reduction of 11.29 PPL and a relative reduction of 10.42% ([Document 1](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m| Comparison | Baseline PPL | Proposed PPL | Absolute Reduction | Relative Reduction |
[0m
[32m|---|---|---|---|---|
[0m
[32m| GRURNTN vs. GRURNN | 97.78 | 87.38 | 10.40 | 10.63% |
[0m
[32m| LSTMRNTN vs. LSTMRNN | 108.26 | 96.97 | 11.29 | 10.42% |

[0m
[32mSource: test set perplexity values as reported in [Document 1](document_1.txt); absolute and relative reductions as stated in the same source.

[0m
[32mTwo observations follow from these paired results. First, the relative gains are nearly identical across the two architecture families — 10.63% for the GRU variant and 10.42% for the LSTM variant — which is consistent with the proposed modification conferring a roughly proportional benefit irrespective of the underlying recurrent unit. Second, in absolute terms LSTMRNTN achieves the larger reduction (11.29 PPL), but this partly reflects its higher starting point; in relative terms GRURNTN's gain is marginally larger. Neither difference in relative gain is large, and the source does not report variance or significance testing, so the two relative gains should be treated as effectively comparable rather than ranked.

[0m
[32mThe source also notes that LSTMRNTN "improved the LSTMRNN model and its performance closely resembles the baseline GRURNN" ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). Numerically, LSTMRNTN (96.97) is 0.81 PPL better than GRURNN (97.78), a relative edge of approximately 0.83%. In other words, the LSTM-based proposed model essentially closes the gap that separated the LSTM baseline from the GRU baseline, but does not open a new margin beyond it. By contrast, the source states that GRURNTN "outperformed all the baseline models as well as the other models by a large margin" ([Document 1](document_1.txt)), and this claim is supported by the cross-model ranking presented below.

[0m
[32m### Improvements Over Previously Published Models

[0m
[32mThe published reference models span a range of 33.5 PPL, from N-Gram at 141 to DOT(S)-RNN at 107.5 ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). Against this set, GRURNTN records large reductions across the board, while LSTMRNTN records moderate but consistent reductions. The table below reports absolute differences and relative reductions computed from the perplexity values given in the source.

[0m
[32m| Prior Model | Prior PPL | GRURNTN PPL | Abs. Gain (GRURNTN) | Rel. Gain (GRURNTN) | LSTMRNTN PPL | Abs. Gain (LSTMRNTN) | Rel. Gain (LSTMRNTN) |
[0m
[32m|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
[0m
[32m| N-Gram | 141 | 87.38 | 53.62 | 38.03% | 96.97 | 44.03 | 31.23% |
[0m
[32m| RNNLM (w/o dyn. eval.) | 124.7 | 87.38 | 37.32 | 29.93% | 96.97 | 27.73 | 22.24% |
[0m
[32m| RNNLM (w/ dyn. eval.) | 123.2 | 87.38 | 35.82 | 29.07% | 96.97 | 26.23 | 21.29% |
[0m
[32m| SCRNN | 115 | 87.38 | 27.62 | 24.02% | 96.97 | 18.03 | 15.68% |
[0m
[32m| sRNN | 110.0 | 87.38 | 22.62 | 20.56% | 96.97 | 13.03 | 11.85% |
[0m
[32m| DOT(S)-RNN | 107.5 | 87.38 | 20.12 | 18.72% | 96.97 | 10.53 | 9.80% |
[0m
[32m| GRURNN (baseline) | 97.78 | 87.38 | 10.40 | 10.63% | 96.97 | 0.81 | 0.83% |
[0m
[32m| LSTMRNN (baseline) | 108.26 | 87.38 | 20.88 | 19.29% | 96.97 | 11.29 | 10.42% |

[0m
[32mSource: perplexity values from [Document 1](document_1.txt); absolute and relative gains computed by the author of this report from those values, using (prior PPL − proposed PPL) / prior PPL × 100.

[0m
[32mSeveral patterns emerge. GRURNTN's advantage over the strongest published prior model, DOT(S)-RNN, is 20.12 PPL, or 18.72% relative; over the weakest, N-Gram, it is 53.62 PPL, or 38.03% relative. LSTMRNTN's corresponding gains are 10.53 PPL (9.80%) over DOT(S)-RNN and 44.03 PPL (31.23%) over N-Gram. Notably, the GRURNTN improvement over the LSTM baseline (19.29% relative) is nearly as large as its improvement over the strongest published model (18.72% relative), because the LSTMRNN baseline itself was weaker than DOT(S)-RNN at 108.26 versus 107.5 PPL ([Document 1](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Cross-Model Ranking

[0m
[32mOrdering all ten reported systems from lowest to highest test perplexity clarifies the overall landscape: GRURNTN (87.38) ranks first, followed by LSTMRNTN (96.97), GRURNN (97.78), DOT(S)-RNN (107.5), LSTMRNN (108.26), sRNN (110.0), SCRNN (115), RNNLM with dynamic evaluation (123.2), RNNLM without dynamic evaluation (124.7), and N-Gram (141) ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). Two implications are worth stating explicitly. First, the GRURNN baseline already surpassed every published model in the comparison set, which means the proposed GRURNTN improves upon a baseline that was already best in class. Second, the LSTMRNN baseline did not surpass the strongest published model, so the proposed LSTMRNTN's advance past DOT(S)-RNN is a genuine, if modest, contribution of that model variant.

[0m
[32m### Training Dynamics

[0m
[32mBeyond terminal test perplexity, the source reports that Fig. 9 compares validation-set perplexity per epoch across models. GRURNN made faster progress than LSTMRNN, and GRURNTN's progress was also better than LSTMRNTN's; the best model in the task was GRURNTN, which had a consistently lower perplexity than the other models throughout training ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). This convergence evidence suggests that GRURNTN's final advantage is not solely a test-set artifact but is observable on held-out validation data across epochs.

[0m
[32m## Discussion and Interpretation

[0m
[32mThe headline answer to the query is that the introduced models achieve approximately a 10.4% to 10.6% relative perplexity reduction over their own direct baselines, and substantially larger reductions — ranging from roughly 9.8% to 38.0% — over previously published models, depending on which prior system is used as the comparator ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). The single most defensible framing of the result is that GRURNTN is the strongest model reported in the study, at 87.38 PPL, beating every baseline and every published comparator by a margin the source describes as large; LSTMRNTN is a moderate improvement that lifts the LSTM family to approximately parity with the GRU baseline but does not exceed GRURNTN.

[0m
[32mThe consistency of the paired relative gains (10.63% and 10.42%) is arguably the most robust finding, because it holds under a controlled comparison in which baselines and proposed models were trained within the same experimental pipeline. The comparisons against published results are directionally clear but weaker evidentially, because they compare numbers produced under potentially different training regimes, regularization schemes, and tuning budgets. The source does not state that published models were re-implemented under identical conditions, so those gaps should be interpreted as indicative rather than strictly controlled.

[0m
[32m## Limitations and Caveats

[0m
[32mSeveral limitations constrain the strength of the conclusions. The evidence rests on a single dataset and task (PTB word-level language modeling), so generalization to other corpora or tasks is untested in the provided material ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). No variance estimates, confidence intervals, or significance tests are reported, so the reliability of small differences — most notably the 0.81 PPL edge of LSTMRNTN over GRURNN — cannot be assessed. The excerpt also omits parameter counts, computational cost, and architectural detail, which are necessary for judging whether the gains are attributable to the proposed mechanism rather than to increased capacity. Finally, the published-model comparisons are cross-study and therefore potentially confounded, and the two RNNLM variants differ by only 1.5 PPL, indicating that the dynamic-evaluation distinction is a comparatively small factor in this comparison set ([Document 1](document_1.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Conclusion

[0m
[32mBased on the reported evidence, the proposed models deliver consistent and meaningful improvements. GRURNTN reduces perplexity by 10.40 absolute points (10.63% relative) over the GRURNN baseline, and LSTMRNTN reduces perplexity by 11.29 absolute points (10.42% relative) over the LSTMRNN baseline ([Document 1](document_1.txt)). Against previously published models, GRURNTN achieves relative reductions of 18.72% against DOT(S)-RNN and 38.03% against N-Gram, while LSTMRNTN achieves 9.80% and 31.23% against the same comparators, respectively. GRURNTN is unambiguously the best-performing system in the study, both in final test perplexity and in per-epoch validation progress, whereas LSTMRNTN provides a moderate gain that essentially brings the LSTM family to parity with the GRU baseline. The improvements are therefore best characterized as robust and practically meaningful within this benchmark, but supported by a narrow evidence base that would benefit from multi-seed replication, significance testing, and efficiency reporting before broader claims can be made.

[0m

[32m# Datasets Used in Parkinson's Disease Speech Analysis: A Report on the German and Czech Corpora

[0m
[32m## Introduction

[0m
[32mThe question of which datasets underpin a given body of Parkinson's disease (PD) speech research is fundamental to evaluating that research's scope, generalizability, and reproducibility. In the present case, the available source material identifies two distinct speech corpora that supply recordings from individuals diagnosed with Parkinson's disease alongside healthy controls (HC): a German dataset and a Czech dataset ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Together, these two resources provide cross-linguistic speech data drawn from native speakers of German and Czech respectively, and they constitute the entirety of the dataset base described in the source document ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32mThis report details the composition of each dataset, compares them on the dimensions for which figures are available, examines the methodological implications of their design, and offers a reasoned assessment of what these corpora can and cannot support. It is important to note at the outset that the source document is a concise descriptive passage rather than a full data descriptor; consequently, certain technical attributes that would ordinarily accompany a comprehensive dataset report — recording conditions, sampling rates, speech tasks, diagnostic criteria, or demographic stratification — are not specified in the material provided ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The analysis below is therefore confined to the parameters that the source explicitly reports, and interpretive claims are appropriately hedged.

[0m
[32m## Primary Datasets Identified

[0m
[32mThe source states plainly that two datasets are used. The first is the German dataset, which "contains speech recordings of 88 PD patients and 88 HC speakers from Germany" ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The second is the Czech dataset, which "contains a total of 100 native Czech speakers, comprising 50 PD patients and 50 healthy controls" ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The source further summarizes these figures by noting that "the speaker counts for the German and Czech data are 88 PD patients with 88 HC speakers and 50 PD patients with 50 HC, respectively" ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32mTwo datasets, therefore, constitute the full corpus base: one German-language resource and one Czech-language resource, each organized as a matched PD-versus-control comparison ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m### The German Speech Dataset

[0m
[32mThe German dataset comprises 176 speakers in total, evenly divided between 88 individuals with Parkinson's disease and 88 healthy control speakers ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). All speakers are described as originating from Germany, which establishes the linguistic and geographic provenance of the recordings as German ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The perfectly symmetrical 1:1 ratio of patients to controls is a notable design feature: it means that the German corpus imposes no class imbalance at the level of speaker counts, a property that is advantageous for any analysis, classification, or statistical comparison that treats PD status as the outcome or grouping variable ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32mIt is worth emphasizing that the German corpus is, on the reported figures, the larger of the two datasets by speaker count, containing 176 speakers against the Czech corpus's 100 ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). This represents a difference of 76 speakers, or roughly 76 percent more participants in the German dataset relative to the Czech one ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m### The Czech Speech Dataset

[0m
[32mThe Czech dataset contains a total of 100 native Czech speakers, split into 50 PD patients and 50 healthy controls ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Two aspects of this description deserve attention. First, the explicit characterization of the speakers as "native Czech" speakers ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)) foregrounds linguistic nativeness as a controlled variable, which is relevant because speech-based analyses of Parkinson's disease often depend on phonetic and prosodic characteristics that vary with native language background. Second, like the German corpus, the Czech dataset exhibits a perfectly balanced case–control ratio of 1:1 ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)), meaning both corpora share the same fundamental design principle of equal allocation between the clinical and control groups.

[0m
[32m## Comparative Overview

[0m
[32mThe structural data reported for the two datasets can be summarized as follows.

[0m
[32m| Dataset | Language / Origin | PD Patients | Healthy Controls | Total Speakers | PD:HC Ratio |
[0m
[32m|---|---|---|---|---|---|
[0m
[32m| German | German (speakers from Germany) | 88 | 88 | 176 | 1:1 |
[0m
[32m| Czech | Czech (native Czech speakers) | 50 | 50 | 100 | 1:1 |
[0m
[32m| Combined | German + Czech | 138 | 138 | 276 | 1:1 |

[0m
[32mAll figures in the table above are derived directly from the source description ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). When the two datasets are considered jointly, the total corpus comprises 276 speakers, of whom 138 are Parkinson's disease patients and 138 are healthy controls ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The aggregate PD-to-control ratio therefore remains exactly 1:1, preserving the balance observed in each constituent dataset ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32mThis aggregate balance is a meaningful property. In many clinical speech studies, control recruitment outpaces or lags patient recruitment, producing imbalanced groups that complicate sensitivity and specificity estimates. Here, the source reports a design in which every dataset is internally balanced, and the pooled data inherit that balance ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Key Characteristics and Design Features

[0m
[32m### Balanced Case–Control Design

[0m
[32mThe most conspicuous shared feature of the two datasets is their strict case–control symmetry. The German dataset pairs each of 88 PD patients with a corresponding control count of 88, while the Czech dataset pairs each of 50 PD patients with 50 controls ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). This is not merely a numerical curiosity; it indicates a deliberate recruitment design in which the clinical and comparison groups were assembled at equivalent scale. From an analytical standpoint, balanced group sizes simplify the interpretation of group-level comparisons and reduce the risk that observed differences are artifacts of unequal sampling ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Cross-Linguistic Scope

[0m
[32mThe second defining feature is the cross-linguistic nature of the combined corpus. The source explicitly frames the datasets as supplying "speech recordings of Parkinson's disease patients and healthy controls across multiple languages" ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The two languages represented are German and Czech ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). This matters because it permits research questions about whether speech markers associated with Parkinson's disease replicate across languages with different phonological and prosodic systems, rather than being confined to a single linguistic population ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Uniform Modality

[0m
[32mBoth datasets are described as containing speech recordings specifically ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The source does not indicate the inclusion of other modalities such as handwriting, gait, or imaging data; the described content is auditory–speech in nature ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Consequently, the corpus base appears to be a speech-focused resource rather than a multimodal one.

[0m
[32m## What These Datasets Enable

[0m
[32mTaken together, the German and Czech corpora as described support a specific set of research activities. First, they permit within-language comparisons of speech characteristics between PD patients and healthy controls, since each dataset contains both groups ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Second, they permit cross-language comparison, because the same case–control structure is replicated in two distinct language communities ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Third, with 276 total speakers, the combined corpus offers a sample size that, while moderate, is sufficient for group-level analyses and for the development of speech-based classification models, particularly given the balanced group sizes ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32mThe replication of the PD-versus-HC contrast across two languages is, in my assessment, the single most scientifically valuable attribute of this dataset pairing. A finding that emerges independently in both the German and the Czech data is substantially more credible than a finding observed in one language alone, because it is less likely to reflect language-specific phonetic idiosyncrasies ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Conversely, a divergence between the two datasets would itself be informative, pointing toward language-dependent or population-dependent effects in how Parkinson's disease manifests in speech.

[0m
[32m## Gaps and Interpretive Limits

[0m
[32mA rigorous report must also state what the source does not establish. The provided description does not specify the recording environment, the microphone or acquisition hardware, the sampling rate, the speech tasks administered (for example, sustained vowels, read passages, or spontaneous speech), the severity distribution of the PD participants, the diagnostic criteria applied, or the demographic matching of age and sex between patients and controls ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Nor does it report the sex or age composition of either dataset ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). These omissions constrain how far the datasets can be evaluated purely on the basis of the source text.

[0m
[32mAccordingly, while the headline figures — 88 PD and 88 HC in the German data, 50 PD and 50 HC in the Czech data, 276 speakers in total — are clearly specified ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)), claims about recording quality, task diversity, or demographic control cannot be validated from the information provided and should not be asserted.

[0m
[32m## Assessment

[0m
[32mOn the evidence supplied, my considered view is that this dataset pairing represents a well-balanced, cross-linguistic speech resource for Parkinson's disease research, with its principal strengths being exact case–control symmetry in both corpora and the availability of two independent language populations ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Its principal limitation, as far as the source reveals, is the absence of accompanying technical and demographic metadata, which prevents a full appraisal of data quality and comparability between the two corpora ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). The German dataset contributes the larger share of speakers at 176, while the Czech dataset contributes 100, yielding a combined total of 276 speakers and 138 PD cases overall ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Any downstream study relying on these data should therefore treat the reported speaker counts as reliable and well-established, while treating unreported acquisition and participant characteristics as unknown pending fuller documentation.

[0m
[32m## Conclusion

[0m
[32mIn direct answer to the query, the datasets used are two: a German speech dataset containing 88 Parkinson's disease patients and 88 healthy controls, and a Czech speech dataset containing 50 Parkinson's disease patients and 50 healthy controls among a total of 100 native Czech speakers ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). Both are speech-recording datasets, both are internally balanced at a 1:1 patient-to-control ratio, and together they span two languages and 276 speakers, comprising 138 PD patients and 138 healthy controls in aggregate ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)). These two corpora, and only these two, constitute the dataset foundation described in the source ([Document_2.txt, n.d.](document_2.txt)).

[32m# Test Set Size in "On NMT Search Errors and Model Errors: Cat Got Your Tongue?"

[0m
[32m## Executive Summary
[0m
[32mThe query "what is the test set size?" has a context-dependent answer in the provided source materials. The full English-German WMT news-test2015 test set used for the paper's main experiments contains 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). However, the length-constrained exact search experiments do not use the full test set; instead, they use subsets. One experiment uses 73.0% of the test set, while other experiments use 48.3% ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). These subset experiments are explicitly distinct from the full test set size of 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). Therefore, the most accurate response is: the full test set size is 2,169 sentences, but the effective test set size for length-constrained exact search ranges from 48.3% to 73.0% of that total, depending on the experiment. This report explains the evidence, distinguishes between full and subset sizes, and provides guidance for interpreting and reporting the test set size.

[0m
[32m## Source Context and Reliability
[0m
[32mThe information provided comes from two document excerpts: document_1, which contains text from a "Results with Length Constraints" section, and document_2, which contains both third-party research notes and excerpts about test set size. Document_1 appears to be a direct excerpt from the paper's results section, while document_2 includes a third-party research note and repeated statements about the paper's test set. The paper's main experiments use the English-German WMT news-test2015 test set, and the test set size is stated as a number of sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The full test set size is reported before any subset selection, which is an important methodological detail because it prevents confusion between the complete evaluation set and the smaller sets used for runtime-constrained experiments ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The source note also lists key questions such as "NMT search errors model errors test set size," "English German news test set size neural machine translation," "WMT news test set sentences exact inference NMT," "Stahlberg Byrne NMT search errors test set size," and "exact inference neural sequence models test set English German" ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). These key questions confirm that the test set size is a central methodological parameter in the paper's evaluation of exact inference for neural machine translation.

[0m
[32m## The Full Test Set Size: 2,169 Sentences
[0m
[32mThe entire English-German WMT news-test2015 test set contains 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). This figure is reported as the full test set size before any subset selection ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The paper's main experiments use this test set, and the language pair is English-German ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). Furthermore, all experiments in the "results without length constraints" section are conducted on the entire English-German WMT news-test2015 test set of 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The paper's use of the entire test set in that section establishes the full test set size for the reported exact inference results ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)).

[0m
[32m### The Full Test Set as the Primary Evaluation Set
[0m
[32mWhen the question "what is the test set size?" refers to the paper's primary or full evaluation, the answer is unambiguously 2,169 sentences. This number is the baseline against which any subset should be compared. It is also the number that should be cited when describing the paper's main exact inference comparisons for neural machine translation search errors and model errors ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The full test set supports the paper's exact inference comparisons, and the source explicitly connects the entire test set to the results without length constraints ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). Thus, for the main experimental results, the test set size is 2,169 sentences.

[0m
[32m### Why 2,169 Is the Authoritative Full Size
[0m
[32mIt is worth emphasizing that 2,169 sentences is not merely an approximate or incidental figure. The source states that the entire test set contains 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The source also states that the full test set size is reported as 2,169 sentences before any subset selection ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). This repetition across the provided excerpts reinforces that 2,169 is the authoritative full test set size. In evaluation practice, reporting the full test set size before subsetting is important because it allows readers to understand the maximum available evaluation data and to assess the representativeness of any later subsampling. The paper's explicit separation of full-set results from subset results supports a transparent reading of its experimental design.

[0m
[32m## Subset Sizes in Length-Constrained Exact Search
[0m
[32mThe length-constrained exact search experiments use only a subset of the test set ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The paper reports that all results in the "results with length constraints" section are conducted on only a subset of the test set to keep the runtime under control ([Document 1, n.d.](#document_1); [Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The reason for the increased runtime is that constraining exact search to certain translation lengths increases the run time because the gamma-bounds are lower ([Document 1, n.d.](#document_1)). In other words, the length-constrained analysis is computationally more expensive, so the authors reduce the evaluation set size to make the experiments feasible.

[0m
[32m### Reported Subset Percentages
[0m
[32mThe provided excerpts state that one experiment uses 73.0% of the test set, while other experiments use 48.3% of the test set ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). These subset percentages are reported specifically for the length-constrained exact search experiments, and they are distinct from the full test set size of 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The source does not report the subsets as absolute sentence counts; it reports them as percentages of the full test set.

[0m

[0m

The approximate sentence counts for the subsets are derived by multiplying the full test set size of 2,169 by the reported percentages. The paper itself reports the subsets as percentages, not as absolute sentence counts, so the derived counts should be labeled as approximations.

[0m
[32mThe approximate counts are calculated as follows: 2,169 × 0.73 = 1,583.37, which rounds to approximately 1,583 sentences, and 2,169 × 0.483 = 1,047.63, which rounds to approximately 1,048 sentences. These calculations are not stated in the source; they are derived from the source's reported percentages and full test set size. The source explicitly reports the percentages and the full size, so the arithmetic is a straightforward inference. The key point is that neither 73.0% nor 48.3% represents the full test set size. They are effective test set sizes for specific length-constrained experiments.

[0m
[32m## Why the Distinction Between Full and Subset Test Set Sizes Matters
[0m
[32mThe distinction between the full test set and the subset test set is not a minor detail. It affects how the paper's results should be interpreted and cited. The full test set of 2,169 sentences supports the paper's exact inference comparisons for neural machine translation search errors and model errors ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). When the paper reports results without length constraints, it uses the entire test set, so those results are based on 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). When the paper reports results with length constraints, it uses subsets, so those results are based on 73.0% or 48.3% of the test set ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)).

[0m
[32m### Implications for Statistical Precision
[0m
[32mFrom a statistical perspective, evaluation on a smaller subset can affect the precision of estimated differences between search errors and model errors. The full test set of 2,169 sentences offers more data than the 48.3% subset and the 73.0% subset. The source does not report variance estimates or confidence intervals for the subset experiments, so this report cannot quantify the loss of statistical power. However, the source does establish that the full test set is used for the main exact inference comparisons and that the length-constrained experiments are deliberately reduced to control runtime ([Document 1, n.d.](#document_1); [Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). Therefore, the most defensible interpretation is that the full test set size is 2,169 sentences, while the length-constrained experiments have smaller effective test set sizes of 73.0% and 48.3% of that total.

[0m
[32m### Implications for Citation Practice
[0m
[32mIf a reader were to state that "the test set size is 2,169 sentences" in the context of a length-constrained result, that statement would be imprecise because the actual evaluation used fewer sentences. Conversely, if a reader were to state that "the test set size is 48.3% of 2,169" without qualification, that statement would incorrectly suggest that the full test set itself is smaller than it is. The source's explicit statement that the subset experiments are "distinct from the full test set size of 2,169 sentences" is a strong signal that the authors and the third-party note want to avoid exactly this kind of confusion ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The paper reports the full test set size before any subset selection, which provides a clean baseline ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). The length-constrained section then applies a runtime-control measure, and the subset percentages are reported so that readers know the effective sample size for those specific analyses ([Document 1, n.d.](#document_1); [Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). This two-level reporting—full size first, subset size second—is methodologically sound because it preserves transparency while acknowledging computational constraints.

[0m
[32m## Recommended Answer and Reporting Guidance
[0m
[32mWhen asked "what is the test set size?" the answer should be given with context. For the paper's full test set and for the results without length constraints, the test set size is 2,169 sentences ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). For the length-constrained exact search experiments, the effective test set size is one of two subsets: 73.0% of the test set or 48.3% of the test set ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)). If approximate sentence counts are needed, these correspond to approximately 1,583 sentences and 1,048 sentences, respectively, based on the full size of 2,169 sentences. The source itself reports the subsets as percentages rather than absolute counts, so any absolute count derived from those percentages should be labeled as approximate.

[0m
[32mA precise response to the query would be: "The full test set size is 2,169 sentences. The length-constrained exact search experiments use subsets of this full test set: one experiment uses 73.0% of the test set, and other experiments use 48.3% of the test set. Therefore, the effective test set size in those length-constrained experiments is smaller than 2,169 sentences." This formulation acknowledges both the full test set size and the subset sizes without conflating them. It also follows the source's own structure, which reports the full test set size before any subset selection and then reports the subset percentages for the length-constrained analyses ([Document 2, n.d.](#document_2)).

[32m# Test Set Size in "On NMT Search Errors and Model Errors: Cat Got Your Tongue?"

[32m## Introduction

[0m
[32mThe query "what is the test set size?" appears straightforward, but the available information reveals an important methodological distinction in the paper "On NMT Search Errors and Model Errors: Cat Got Your Tongue?" The paper reports a full test set size of 3,003 sentences for its main experiments, yet it also uses reduced subsets for a specific set of length-constrained exact search experiments. This report synthesizes the provided sources, document_1.txt and document_2.txt, to answer the query with precision. The central finding is that the full test set size is 3,003 sentences, specifically the English-German WMT news-test2015 test set. However, the length-constrained exact search experiments use only subsets of this test set: one experiment uses 73.0% of the test set, while other experiments use 48.3% of the test set. These subset sizes are distinct from the full test set size and are reported to keep runtime under control. The report will first establish the full test set size, then examine the length-constrained subsets, compare them in a structured table, discuss methodological implications, and provide a direct answer to the query.

[0m
[32m## The Full Test Set Size: 3,003 Sentences

[0m
[32mAccording to a third-party research note on the paper, the main experiments use the entire English-German WMT news-test2015 test set of 3,003 sentences ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The entire English-German WMT news-test2015 test set contains 3,003 sentences, and the paper reports this size in its experimental setup ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The paper states the test set size as a number of sentences, not as a character count, token count, or any other unit ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). This full test set size of 3,003 sentences is reported before any subset selection ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The test set is a news test set, and the language pair is English-German ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The third-party note explicitly addresses key questions such as "NMT search errors model errors test set size," "English German news test set size neural machine translation," "WMT news test set sentences exact inference NMT," "Stahlberg Byrne NMT search errors test set size," and "exact inference neural sequence models test set English German," indicating that the 3,003-sentence figure is central to understanding the paper's experimental design ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32mThe paper also reports that all experiments in the results without length constraints section are conducted on the entire English-German WMT news-test2015 test set (3,003 sentences) ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). This full test set supports the paper's exact inference comparisons for neural machine translation search errors and model errors ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The use of the entire test set in that section establishes the full test set size for the reported exact inference results ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). In other words, when the paper presents results without length constraints, the evaluation is performed on all 3,003 sentences. This is the baseline against which the length-constrained experiments should be compared. The full test set size is therefore not merely an incidental detail but a foundational element of the paper's main empirical claims.

[0m
[32m### Why the Full Test Set Size Matters

[0m
[32mThe 3,003-sentence test set is the reference point for the paper's primary findings on NMT search errors and model errors. Because the paper's main experiments use the entire test set, any general statement about the paper's test set size should default to 3,003 sentences unless the length-constrained section is specifically referenced ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The full test set size is reported before any subset selection, which means that the subsets used later are not a redefinition of the original test set but rather a sampling from it ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). This distinction is crucial for reproducibility: a researcher attempting to replicate the paper's main results must use all 3,003 sentences, while a researcher replicating the length-constrained results must use the appropriate subset. The third-party research note emphasizes that the full test set size is 3,003 sentences and that this is the size used for the paper's main experiments ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m## Subsets for Length-Constrained Exact Search

[0m
[32m### Rationale for Subsetting

[0m
[32mThe length-constrained exact search experiments use only a subset of the test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The paper reports that all results in the results with length constraints section are conducted on only a subset of the test set to keep the runtime under control ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The rationale is explicitly stated in document_1.txt: constraining exact search to certain translation lengths increases the run time as the gamma-bounds are lower ([document_1.txt](document_1.txt)). Therefore, all results in that section are conducted on only a subset of the test set to keep the runtime under control ([document_1.txt](document_1.txt)). This is a deliberate methodological trade-off. Exact search in neural machine translation is computationally expensive, and imposing length constraints further exacerbates the cost because lower gamma-bounds reduce the efficiency of the search procedure. To make the experiments feasible, the authors reduce the evaluation set size. This decision is transparently reported, which is important for interpreting the results.

[0m
[32m### Specific Subset Proportions

[0m
[32mOne experiment uses 73.0% of the test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Other experiments use 48.3% of the test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The paper reports these subsets for the length-constrained exact search experiments ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). These subset experiments are distinct from the full test set size of 3,003 sentences ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The paper reports the full test set size as 3,003 sentences before any subset selection ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The two proportions, 73.0% and 48.3%, indicate that the length-constrained section does not use a single uniform test set size. Instead, the evaluation size varies by experiment. This variation likely reflects differences in computational cost across different length constraints or search configurations. The paper does not provide absolute sentence counts for these subsets in the provided information; it reports them as percentages of the full test set.

[0m
[32m### Calculated Approximate Sentence Counts

[0m
[32mUsing the full test set size of 3,003 sentences and the reported percentages, the subsets correspond to approximately the following sentence counts:

[0m
[32m- 73.0% of 3,003 = 2,192.19, which is approximately 2,192 sentences.
[0m
[32m- 48.3% of 3,003 = 1,450.449, which is approximately 1,450 sentences.

[0m
[32mThese calculations are derived from the figures provided in document_2.txt ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The paper itself reports the proportions as percentages, so the exact sentence counts may depend on rounding or the specific subset selection procedure. However, these approximations illustrate the reduction in evaluation size. For the 73.0% subset, the reduction from 3,003 to approximately 2,192 sentences represents a decrease of about 811 sentences. For the 48.3% subset, the reduction to approximately 1,450 sentences represents a decrease of about 1,553 sentences. These are substantial reductions that could affect statistical power and the precision of estimated effects. Nevertheless, the subsets were chosen to balance computational feasibility with the need for empirical evidence.

[32m| Full test set / main experiments / results without length constraints | 3,003 sentences (100%) | ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)) |
[0m
[32m| Length-constrained exact search – one experiment | 73.0% of test set (approx. 2,192 sentences) | ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)) |
[0m
[32m| Length-constrained exact search – other experiments | 48.3% of test set (approx. 1,450 sentences) | ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)) |

[0m
[32mThe table above summarizes the key figures. The full test set size is 3,003 sentences, as reported in the paper's experimental setup and confirmed by the third-party research note ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The length-constrained exact search experiments use subsets of this full test set, with one experiment at 73.0% and others at 48.3% ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). These subset sizes are not the test set size for the paper as a whole; they are specific to the length-constrained section.

[0m
[32m## Methodological and Interpretive Considerations

[0m
[32mThe distinction between the full test set and the length-constrained subsets has several important implications. First, the full test set size of 3,003 sentences is the reference point for the paper's main and non-length-constrained experiments ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Second, the length-constrained results are not directly comparable to the full-test-set results because they use only a fraction of the data ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Third, the paper explicitly states that the subsets were used to keep runtime under control, which means that the reduction in test set size is a deliberate methodological choice rather than an error ([document_1.txt](document_1.txt); [document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Fourth, the use of different subset proportions (73.0% versus 48.3%) suggests that the computational cost varies across length-constrained experiments, likely due to different translation length constraints or search configurations ([document_1.txt](document_1.txt); [document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Fifth, when reproducing or comparing results, researchers must verify which subset was used for each specific length-constrained experiment ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Sixth, the full test set size of 3,003 sentences is reported before any subset selection, which means that the subsets are not a redefinition of the original test set but rather a sampling from it ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Seventh, the paper's main experiments use the entire test set, so any claim about the paper's overall test set size should default to 3,003 sentences unless the length-constrained section is specifically referenced ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Eighth, the third-party research note emphasizes the English-German WMT news-test2015 test set, confirming the language pair and domain ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Ninth, the exact inference comparisons for NMT search errors and model errors rely on the full test set, which strengthens the generalizability of those findings ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Tenth, the length-constrained experiments, while using subsets, still provide valuable insights into length deficiency, but their results should be interpreted with the reduced sample size in mind ([document_1.txt](document_1.txt); [document_2.txt](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Potential Misinterpretations

[0m
[32mA common misinterpretation would be to read the length-constrained section and conclude that the paper's test set size is 73.0% or 48.3% of 3,003 sentences. That would be incorrect for the paper as a whole. The full test set size is 3,003 sentences, and the subsets are used only for the length-constrained exact search experiments ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Another misinterpretation would be to assume that all length-constrained experiments use the same subset size. The provided information shows that one experiment uses 73.0% and other experiments use 48.3% ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Therefore, the test set size in the length-constrained section is not a single number but a function of the specific experiment. A third misinterpretation would be to treat the percentages as exact sentence counts. The paper reports percentages, not absolute counts for the subsets. The approximate sentence counts derived in this report are calculations based on the full test set size and are provided for illustrative purposes only. The authoritative figures are the full test set size of 3,003 sentences and the subset proportions of 73.0% and 48.3% ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)).

[0m
[32m### Reproducibility and Reporting Standards

[0m
[32mThe paper's explicit reporting of subset proportions is a positive example of transparent methodological reporting. By stating that all results in the length-constrained section are conducted on only a subset of the test set to keep runtime under control, the authors allow readers to understand the trade-offs involved ([document_1.txt](document_1.txt)). The paper also reports the full test set size as 3,003 sentences before any subset selection, which provides a clear baseline ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). This is important because it allows other researchers to calculate the approximate subset sizes if needed. However, for full reproducibility, it would be ideal if the paper also reported the exact number of sentences in each subset, not just the percentages. The provided information does not include exact subset sentence counts, so any reproduction attempt would need to infer them from the percentages or consult the original paper's data. Nevertheless, the information available is sufficient to answer the query about the test set size.

[0m
[32m## Answer to the Query

[0m
[32mIf the query "what is the test set size?" refers to the paper's overall or main experimental setup, the answer is 3,003 sentences, specifically the English-German WMT news-test2015 test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). This is the full test set size that is reported in the paper's experimental setup and used for the main experiments and the results without length constraints ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). If the query refers to the length-constrained exact search experiments, the answer is not a single number: one experiment uses 73.0% of the test set, and other experiments use 48.3% of the test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The full test set size remains 3,003 sentences before any subset selection ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Therefore, the most complete answer is: the full test set size is 3,003 sentences, while length-constrained exact search experiments use subsets of 73.0% and 48.3% of that full test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt); [document_1.txt](document_1.txt)). This dual answer reflects the paper's methodological design and prevents oversimplification.

[0m
[32m## Conclusion

[0m
[32mThis report has synthesized the available information to answer the query about the test set size in "On NMT Search Errors and Model Errors: Cat Got Your Tongue?" The paper reports a full test set size of 3,003 sentences for the English-German WMT news-test2015 test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). The main experiments and the results without length constraints use this entire test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). In contrast, the length-constrained exact search experiments use subsets of the test set to manage runtime, with one experiment using 73.0% and other experiments using 48.3% of the test set ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt); [document_1.txt](document_1.txt)). These subsets are distinct from the full test set size and are reported as proportions rather than absolute sentence counts ([document_2.txt](document_2.txt)). Based on the provided information, the definitive answer to "what is the test set size?" is 3,003 sentences for the full test set, with the caveat that length-constrained experiments use reduced subsets. This distinction is crucial for accurate interpretation and reproduction of the paper's results. Future work should report both absolute and relative subset sizes to facilitate direct comparison and replication.
